- **Two build configurations must each reach 100% independently, not be merged into one number.** `internal/analytics` compiles `stub.go` under the default build and `duckdb.go` under `-tags duckdb` — different files, different statement counts (100% vs 80.6% today). `go tool cover` cannot merge profiles from two different file sets into one meaningful percentage; `scripts/coverage-go.sh <default|duckdb>` runs each independently and every downstream number (the ratchet, the eventual hard gate) stays two numbers, not one.
- **Excluded from the Go denominator, defined in `scripts/coverage-exclude-go.txt` (the one authoritative, machine-read list — this entry describes it, the file enforces it):**
  - `internal/applog`'s dialog/opendir split is 3-way (`_darwin.go`, `_windows.go`, `_other.go` via `!darwin && !windows`), not 2-way — all 6 files excluded, symmetrically. This was caught by adversarial review before the first commit: CI (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`) runs `make verify` on `ubuntu-24.04`, where the `_other.go` arm compiles instead of the `_darwin.go` arm this list was first written against on a macOS dev machine. Excluding only the darwin/windows arms (as originally written) would have made the Go coverage denominator platform-dependent — CI's first run would have counted the `_other.go` files (mostly uncovered) that the macOS-recorded baseline never saw, producing a false "REGRESSED" failure on the very first push. All three arms are excluded so the denominator is identical on every host.
  - `internal/templates/jdm_windows.go` — windows-only, excluded. Its sibling `internal/templates/jdm.go` (`!windows`) is **not** excluded: it compiles on both this macOS dev machine and CI's `ubuntu-24.04`, so it's ordinary cross-platform business logic, covered normally like any other file — the platform split here isn't symmetric the way `applog`'s is, and treating it as if it were would wrongly exclude 121 lines of code CI can actually exercise.
  - 6 platform-narrow files total, 219 lines. Genuinely uncoverable on a single-host Go toolchain without cross-GOOS CI; not the same case as the `runtime.GOOS ==` conditionals below.
  - `gopmgr/frontend/node_modules/flatted/golang/pkg/flatted` — vendored third-party Go code `go list ./...` sweeps in incidentally. Excluded at the package-list level in `scripts/coverage-go.sh` (never even compiled for coverage), not just filtered from the profile.
  - `main()`'s literal entrypoint — conventional exclusion for any Go program's bootstrap function; will be documented per-function when Phase 4 (root/App layer) reaches it, not blanket-excluded here.
- **NOT excluded, refactored instead:** the 6 `runtime.GOOS ==` conditionals in `main.go` (4) and `internal/users/store.go` (2, inside `DefaultRootDir`/`legacyRootCandidates` — the exact functions behind the `f1a5501` migration data-loss fix). These compile on every platform; both arms are reachable in a single test run once the OS check sits behind an injectable seam. Checked first whether the 5 excluded platform-suffixed files are *selected by* one of these 6 conditionals (they're not — filename-suffix build tags and `runtime.GOOS` checks don't interact here), so the exclusion list and the seam refactor are independent changes. The seam refactor is scoped as its own commit, sequenced after `internal/cli`/`internal/templates` in Phase 1, specifically because `store.go` is the file this session's data-loss fix (`f1a5501`) landed in — `TestPreRenameInstallIsUsableAfterMigration` and the rest of `internal/users`'s suite must pass unchanged before and after, and the seam itself must be break-verified in both arms.
- **Frontend exclusions:** `frontend/wailsjs/` (generated Wails bindings) — already outside the `src/**` include glob, no action needed. Nothing else currently excluded.
- **Enforcement: a ratchet, not an immediate hard floor — and NOT currently wired into `make verify`.** `scripts/coverage-ratchet.sh` (run manually, or via `make coverage-ratchet`) fails only if any of the three marks (go_default, go_duckdb, frontend) drops below `coverage-baseline.json`'s recorded high-water mark — comparing exact covered/total statement counts as a float ratio, not a rounded percentage, so a one-statement regression is still caught. A hard 100%-required gate would fail on every commit until the very last one of what's estimated (see below) to be a multi-session effort, blocking all unrelated work for the duration; the ratchet lets other work land throughout and converts to a hard floor once 100% is actually reached. It is deliberately **not** a `verify` prerequisite yet: CI's `verify` job (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`) builds with `GO_TEST_TAGS=webkit2_41` only and installs no DuckDB CGO toolchain, so `coverage-go.sh duckdb` would hard-fail there today — a build failure the `GOPMGR_ALLOW_COVERAGE_REGRESSION` override below can't rescue, since that only skips the regression check, not a compile error. Caught by adversarial review before committing (an earlier draft wired it into `verify` and would have broken CI on the first push). Wire it in once CI installs the duckdb toolchain, or in Phase 7 when this converts to a hard floor. `make coverage-ratchet-update` re-runs and records any *improved* marks — run it after adding tests, never to paper over a real regression. Break-verified: a synthetic inflated baseline value correctly failed the ratchet (exit 1, "REGRESSED" reported), restored and confirmed exit 0.
- **`GOPMGR_ALLOW_COVERAGE_REGRESSION`, an explicit developer-set escape hatch, same shape as `GOPMGR_SKIP_NSIS_COMPILE`.** Caught by adversarial review before the first commit: a strict ratchet with no override fails `make verify` on *any* unrelated commit that adds a not-yet-tested line — a bugfix, a small feature, an untested error branch — for the entire multi-session duration of this effort. That's the same "blocks all unrelated work" problem the user chose a ratchet specifically to avoid, just moved one level down. Setting the var skips the fail-on-regression check for one run; it still computes and prints the real numbers, and `--update` still refuses to record a worse mark even with the var set, so a regression can be let through for one commit but can never get silently baked in as the new floor. Break-verified: with a synthetic regression, unset failed (exit 1), set exited 0 while printing "REGRESSED ... not failing" and leaving `coverage-baseline.json` unchanged even under `--update`.
- **The ratchet compares uncovered STATEMENT COUNT, not percentage — changed after the very first real coverage-improving commit tripped a false positive.** `internal/templates`'s cleanup (below) deleted 3 `jdm.go` branches proven dead by type, which removed statements that were already 100% covered. Deleting already-covered statements from a repo whose overall average is well below 100% mathematically *lowers* the percentage (removing above-average-coverage code from a below-average-overall pool always does) — the ratchet reported a "regression" for a change that made the codebase strictly better (less code, none of it untested). Uncovered count (`total - covered`) doesn't have this trap: deleting fully-covered code leaves it unchanged, only adding untested code or losing test coverage increases it, and `uncovered == 0` is an exact terminal condition for 100% with no float comparison. `coverage-baseline.json`'s stored `covered`/`total` fields are unchanged (uncovered is computed at comparison time); only the comparison logic in `coverage-ratchet.sh` changed. Break-verified: the original synthetic-inflated-baseline case (a negative uncovered count) still correctly fails, and this session's real deletion — which had needed `GOPMGR_ALLOW_COVERAGE_REGRESSION` under the old percentage comparison — now holds cleanly with no override. One consequence worth knowing: after a "held" (not "improved") comparison, `coverage-baseline.json`'s stored `covered`/`total` can lag what `coverage-go.sh`/`coverage-frontend.sh` print right now — e.g. after this deletion the file still shows `go_default: 8604/14606` while a fresh run prints `8602/14604`. That's expected, not a bug: `--update` only rewrites on genuine improvement (fewer uncovered), and uncovered count — the field the ratchet actually compares — is unaffected (`14606-8604 = 14604-8602 = 6002`) and self-heals the next time real progress lands. Don't "fix" a stale-looking baseline by hand-editing the file; let `--update` do it when uncovered count actually drops.
- **Cost estimate, derived from measured evidence, not guessed:** `ProjectLaunchpad.svelte` (small, cohesive) reached 91.9% (273/297 statements) with 2 well-aimed tests — ~136 statements/test. `ProjectSettings.svelte` (large, multi-section, already has a test file) sits at 48.2% (680/1,410) after 2 tests covering only its PAdES-timestamp section — most of the codebase's 233 Svelte files are architecturally closer to the latter than the former. Blending both cases: **11,256 uncovered frontend statements ≈ 80–280 additional interaction-tested component tests**, the dominant cost in this effort by roughly two orders of magnitude over the Go gap, and the reason Phase 6 (frontend components) is planned as a multi-session, batched effort rather than one pass.
- **Phase order is ROI-driven, not alphabetical:** `internal/cli` (4.5% today, almost entirely pure string-parsing functions — cheapest statements in the repo) and `internal/templates` (20.7%) come before the 70–99%-covered "completion tier" packages, which come before genuine construction-tier work (`charts/pdfrender`, `documents`, `update`, `db`, `agile`, `export`, `money`, `scripts`, `tools/update-manifest`), which comes before the root/App layer (48.2%, needs Wails-runtime-free test scaffolding), which comes before the frontend. Full phase list and the four scope decisions above were reviewed by an adversarial self-review pass before implementation began, per the user's explicit process for this work.
- **Baseline recorded this session (`coverage-baseline.json`):** go_default 8525/14611 (58.35%), go_duckdb 8603/14709 (58.49%), frontend 1939/13195 (14.69%). Everything past Phase 0 measures against these.

### 2026-08-04 — First wall: the exclusion mechanism is file-granular, `internal/templates` needed line-granular

- **`internal/templates/jdm.go`'s `Evaluate` had 4 defensive branches; adversarial review before writing any test sorted them into three different outcomes, not one.** `json.Marshal(req)` (req is `SeedRequest{Industry, Methodology string}`), the resulting `json.Unmarshal` into `map[string]any`, and `json.Marshal(result.Result)` (a `json.RawMessage`, whose `MarshalJSON` always returns `m, nil` or `"null", nil` — never an error) are all **provably unreachable by type**, not just unreached by today's tests. Per this repo's own rule against handling errors that can't happen, all three were **deleted**, which also removes them from the coverage denominator — no exclusion-list entry needed, because dead code that no longer exists can't be uncovered code. `e.z.Evaluate`'s error path, by contrast, is a real, reachable failure mode (crossing into an external C-bound library) — **tested**, using the exact fixture shape zen-go's own `TestEngine_ErrorTransparency` uses (a loader that returns an error), which exercises this package's own error-wrapping (`fmt.Errorf("templates: evaluate: %w", err)`) rather than pinning the library's internals.
- **The 4th branch, `NewEngine`'s loader "unknown key" case, is where 100% first collided with the tooling committed in `f4c5036`.** It's real, intentional defensive code — the package doc explicitly anticipates "future versions can ship organisation-specific overlay rules in a sibling JDM file," which is exactly the scenario that would call the loader with a second key. It is not dead by type, so it can't be deleted like the other three. But `internal/templates`' single JDM document (`launchpad_seeds.json`) never triggers it, and a search of zen-go's own test fixtures found no node type that causes a second loader call — reaching it would mean hand-crafting a decision graph purely to tick a coverage box, which was already ruled out as testing methodology (pins a fixture production will never produce, not this package's behavior). **`scripts/coverage-exclude-go.txt` is file-granular** (`coverage-go.sh` does `grep -Ev -f` against whole file paths in the profile) — there is no way to exclude one branch inside an otherwise-100%-reachable file without excluding the entire file, which would hide the ~15 statements around it that genuinely are covered.
- **Decision: leave it uncovered and recorded, not force a workaround.** `internal/templates` sits at 97.3% (up from 20.7%) rather than 100%. Extending the exclusion mechanism to line/branch granularity (e.g. `// coverage:ignore` comments parsed by `coverage-go.sh`) is deferred rather than built now, on a sample of one — Phase 2 (15 packages currently at 70–99%) will show whether this is a one-off or a recurring pattern before any tooling investment is justified. If it recurs, extend the mechanism then, with evidence of the real shape of the problem instead of guessing at it here.
- **A second, smaller instance of the same wall:** `seedDocument`'s "unknown document kind" branch is untestable through the public `Apply`/`applyOne` path (all 8 dispatched kinds are confirmed registered in `internal/documents` — checked, not assumed), but *is* directly reachable by calling the unexported `seedDocument` helper with an invalid kind from a same-package test, since its `kind` parameter is a plain string, not type-constrained to the 8 real values. Tested directly. This one didn't hit the wall — worth contrasting with `jdm.go`'s case so a future reader can tell "reachable via a different call path" from "reachable only by fixturing a third-party library's internals" apart.

### 2026-08-05 — Phase 1 complete: `runtime.GOOS` conditionals now covered via an injectable seam

- **The problem this closes:** `runtime.GOOS` is fixed for the lifetime of one compiled test binary — a `go test` run on this macOS dev machine can only ever exercise the `"darwin"` arm of a `runtime.GOOS == "darwin"` conditional, never the other side, no matter how the test is written. Six such conditionals existed: 4 in `main.go`'s `buildAppMenu` (App/Edit/Window menu roles vs. hand-built equivalents, and where the Quit menu item lives), 2 in `internal/users/store.go`'s `DefaultRootDir`/`legacyRootCandidates`. This is different from the file-granular platform split documented in the "100% coverage: scope and exclusions" entry above (`_darwin.go`/`_windows.go`/`_other.go` files, which genuinely can't all compile on one host) — these conditionals compile everywhere, so both arms *are* reachable in a single run once the OS check is behind a parameter instead of a direct `runtime.GOOS` read.
- **The seam pattern used everywhere here: extract a pure helper that takes `goos` as an explicit parameter, keep the public/production entry point reading `runtime.GOOS` and delegating to it.** `internal/users/store.go`: `DefaultRootDir()`/`legacyRootCandidates()` are unchanged in signature (still exported, still zero-argument, still read `runtime.GOOS`) — they now delegate to new unexported `defaultRootDirForGOOS(goos, home string) string` / `legacyRootCandidatesForGOOS(goos, home string) []string`, which tests call directly with both `"darwin"` and a non-darwin value in one test function. `main.go`: `buildAppMenu` gained a `goos string` parameter directly (it's already unexported and has exactly one call site, `buildAppOptions`, which now passes `runtime.GOOS`) — no pure-helper split needed since the whole function was already a reasonable unit to parameterize.
- **Proof the seam actually matters, not just a coverage-number exercise:** for each of the 3 mutations tested against `store.go`'s non-darwin/darwin branches, the *existing* `runtime.GOOS`-dependent tests (`TestDefaultRootDirPlatformDefault_UsesGoPMgrLeaf`, `TestLegacyRootCandidates_PrefersApplicationSupportOverDocuments`) stayed **green** under a mutation to the branch that isn't real-`runtime.GOOS` on this machine (they can only ever exercise the darwin arm here), while the new pure-helper tests correctly went red. That's the concrete, observed failure mode this refactor closes — not hypothetical.
- **`internal/users/store.go` is the file `f1a5501`'s migration-fix landed in.** Every existing test in the package, including `TestPreRenameInstallIsUsableAfterMigration`, was confirmed to pass unchanged both before and after this change — this session's standing rule (own commit, no bundled unrelated changes, existing suite verified around sensitive code) was followed exactly as planned when this item was first deferred from Phase 1.
- **Also closed, adjacent and cheap:** the `os.UserHomeDir()` error branch shared by both `DefaultRootDir` and `legacyRootCandidates` — unrelated to the GOOS seam, but a one-line `t.Setenv("HOME", "")` reliably and hermetically triggers it on POSIX (skipped on Windows, where `os.UserHomeDir` reads different env vars).
- **`main.go`'s `buildAppMenu` moved from 57.9% to 71.1%, not higher — the remaining gap is scoped out on purpose.** The uncovered lines are menu-item `Click` callback *bodies* (the closures that call `wailsruntime.EventsEmit`, `wailsruntime.Quit`, `wailsruntime.WindowToggleMaximise`, `wailsruntime.MessageDialog`, etc.) — these need a live Wails runtime context (`app.ctx`) to exercise meaningfully; calling them with a nil context just hits the existing `if app.ctx != nil` guard and returns without touching the Wails call. Covering these belongs to Phase 4 (root/App layer, which already has a pattern for constructing a Wails-runtime-free `*App`) rather than this narrowly-scoped GOOS-seam task — noted here so a future reader doesn't assume `buildAppMenu` was meant to reach 100% in this pass.
- **Test coverage ledger:** a new `TEST_COVERAGE_LEDGER.md` catalogs every test file in the repo (Go and frontend) with what it covers, how, and why — see that file for the authoritative, itemized inventory. This handbook continues to record *why* non-obvious testing decisions were made; the ledger records *what exists*.

### 2026-08-05 — Phase 2 begins: `internal/applog` to 100%; adversarial review of `a0360b5`; ledger drift enforcement

- **Retroactive adversarial review, per standing process.** `/advisor` had been unavailable for the entire prior session (flagged and disclosed at the time); it recovered this session and reviewed `a0360b5` (the Phase 1 completion commit) before any Phase 2 work started, as promised. Verdict: sound — the seam pattern and its break-verify proof were the strongest evidence in the commit. Two non-blocking findings, both addressed in this same commit rather than deferred:
  1. `TEST_COVERAGE_LEDGER.md`'s `internal/db` row for `backup_test.go` said schema-version dispatch covers "v1 `.pmforge` vs. v2 `.gopmgr` entries" as if one test proved both; the v1 *read* path is specifically pinned by `TestRestoreArchivalBundleReadsSchemaVersion1Archive`, the v2 path by the create/round-trip tests. Row corrected to name the specific tests.
  2. The ledger itself had no enforcement — a 150+-row hand-written document, in a repo where the whole point of this multi-session effort has been "don't trust prose, trust something that fails loudly." Addressed below.
- **Ledger methodology disclosed in the document itself.** Added a "Methodology and confidence" note to `TEST_COVERAGE_LEDGER.md`'s Purpose section: rows are written from test names, source, and doc comments, not fully re-verified against every assertion; a sample was checked directly during this review and held, but any single row should be treated as correctable, not authoritative. Converts an unstated risk into a declared one.
- **`scripts/check-coverage-ledger-current.sh` (new), wired into `make verify` as the `coverage-ledger-current` target.** Deliberately narrow: it only checks that every `*_test.go`/`*.test.ts` file's basename appears somewhere in `TEST_COVERAGE_LEDGER.md` — a presence check, not a content audit. It cannot catch a row that's become inaccurate for a file that still exists (that's what the methodology note above is for), only the more common and more silent failure mode: a new test file added and the ledger never updated, which nothing else in the repo would ever notice. Cheap (pure `find`/`grep`, no Go build, no test execution) so it's safe inside `verify`'s fast pre-commit gate, unlike `coverage-ratchet` which stays out for the DuckDB-toolchain reason recorded above. Prunes by `-name node_modules` (not a hardcoded `./frontend/node_modules` path), so it doesn't fail `verify` for unrelated reasons if a `node_modules` ever appears elsewhere in the tree — the same "don't block unrelated work" concern already applied to the coverage ratchet and the NSIS skip. Break-verified: an untracked `zzz_fake_test.go` dropped into `internal/applog/` was correctly flagged and failed the check; removing it restored a clean pass. Its own limit showed up on first use: the `internal/applog` row this same commit added initially said "15" tests where the file has 14 (an off-by-one from hand-counting, caught by adversarial review, not by this script) — the gate can prove a file is *mentioned*, not that a row's details are *right*, exactly the boundary the methodology note above states.
- **`internal/applog`: 72.5% → 100% of `applog.go`'s own statements (95.7% of the package total; the platform-narrow `dialog_darwin.go`/`opendir_darwin.go` stay excluded per the existing exclusion list).** The gap was concentrated in `Init`'s three "never fail outright" fallback branches and all of `Fatal` — chosen as the Phase 2 starting package per the advisor's guidance (`go tool cover -func` showed its gap was error-path shaped, cheap to close, vs. `internal/crypto`'s gap being spread across PDF/CMS signing fixture construction, materially more expensive per statement; advised finishing one cheap package first to get a real statements-per-hour figure before estimating the rest of the 15-package tier).
  - `Init`'s `MkdirAll`-fails and `OpenFile`-fails branches: forced with real-but-controlled filesystem obstacles — a plain file sitting where the `logs` subdirectory needs to be created (`MkdirAll` returns `ENOTDIR`), and the exact dated log filename Init will try to open pre-created as a directory (`OpenFile` returns "is a directory"). Chosen over `chmod`-based permission denial specifically because tests running as root (a real CI/container condition) bypass Unix permission checks entirely, which would make a permission-based test silently pass for the wrong reason on such a host.
  - `Init`'s "no writable log directory found" branch and `resolveLogDir`'s matching final-fallback logic: `os.TempDir()` always returns a non-empty value on every real POSIX/Windows host (`/tmp` default on Unix even with `TMPDIR` unset), so this branch is genuinely unreachable via environment manipulation on any real machine — the same class of "hard case" flagged for `internal/templates` in the entry above. Rather than leave it undocumented-and-uncovered again, `userHomeDir`/`tempDir` were extracted as package-level func vars (`var userHomeDir = os.UserHomeDir`, `var tempDir = os.TempDir`) — the identical injectable-seam pattern used for `runtime.GOOS` in Phase 1 — so tests can force both to fail and observe the real fallback behavior.
  - `Fatal` needed the same treatment regardless of any filesystem trick: it calls `ShowError` (a real native OS dialog via `osascript` on darwin) and `os.Exit(1)`, neither of which a test can safely call directly. Added `showError`/`osExit` func vars alongside the two above; `Fatal`'s body now calls the vars instead of `ShowError`/`os.Exit` directly, and tests substitute closures that record what was called instead of firing them for real.
  - Break-verified 5 distinct mutations: `Init`'s `MkdirAll`-fails branch, `Init`'s `OpenFile`-fails branch, `Init`'s `logDir == ""` branch, `resolveLogDir`'s temp-fallback branch, and `Fatal`'s `showError` call. All were eventually caught, but 2 of the 5 were **not** caught on the first attempt — worth recording precisely because this session's whole methodology rests on break-verify being trustworthy evidence, and the first pass here wasn't:
    - The `MkdirAll`-fails mutation (removing only the early `return`, leaving the log message in place) fell through into the `OpenFile` branch, which failed for its own reason and produced the identical `logPath == ""` observable — the test as originally written asserted only the outcome, not which branch produced it, so it stayed green under a mutation it was meant to catch.
    - The `logDir == ""` mutation had the identical failure shape: removing that branch let an empty `logDir` fall through to `os.MkdirAll("", dirPerm)`, which also fails and produces the same `logPath == ""`. Same root cause, found by re-running the same class of check against the second branch rather than assuming the first fix generalized.
    - Both fixed the same way: capture the actual log message (a real `os.Pipe()`-based stderr capture, since `Init`'s failure branches call `log.SetOutput(os.Stderr)` themselves and would silently discard a `log.SetOutput(&buf)` done from the test side) and assert on the specific text each branch emits ("cannot create log dir" vs. "no writable log directory found"), which only that branch produces. Re-running the same mutations against the tightened tests then failed correctly.
    - `captureStderr`'s restore is registered with `t.Cleanup` and its returned `stop()` is idempotent — caught in the same review pass: an `os.Stderr`-redirecting helper that only restores via an explicit `defer`/call site leaves the real `os.Stderr` pointed at an unread, unclosed pipe if the test exits early via `t.Fatalf` (which never returns to run code after it), silently swallowing every later test's stderr output in the same package and risking a full pipe buffer blocking a write.
    - `TestInit_OpenFileFails` blocks both today's and tomorrow's dated filename, not just today's: the test computes "today" once to build its filesystem obstacle, then `Init` calls `time.Now()` again itself: a run straddling midnight between the two calls would otherwise flake.
  - `pruneOldLogs`'s `os.ReadDir` error branch was closed directly with a nonexistent directory (no seam needed) but, unlike the other branches above, is disclosed here as non-discriminating rather than genuinely break-verified: `TestPruneOldLogs_UnreadableDirIsANoOp` exercises the branch for the coverage ratchet, but its entire contract is "return early, touch nothing" — deleting that early return leaves `entries` nil, and ranging over a nil slice still returns cleanly with zero iterations, so no assertion this test could make would distinguish the branch running from the branch being deleted. Recorded here rather than left as an implicit gap, per this section's own "treat stale/overstated claims as bugs" standard.
- **Coverage ratchet updated:** go_default 8612/14605 (5993 uncovered) → 8628/14605 (5977 uncovered); go_duckdb 8690/14703 (6013 uncovered) → 8706/14703 (5997 uncovered). frontend unchanged (no frontend files touched this pass).

### 2026-08-05 — `internal/crypto`: a coverage pass on `LoadCertificate` found a real signing bug, fixed first

- **What happened:** planning coverage for `pdf_sign.go`'s two 0% functions (`LoadCertificate`, `SignPDFHash`) required generating a real PKCS#12 fixture, since `LoadCertificate` reads a `.p12` file from disk. Probing `golang.org/x/crypto/pkcs12` with the fixture (see the probe output kept in this entry below) showed `LoadCertificate`'s use of `pkcs12.Decode` hard-requires exactly one private-key bag and one certificate bag — `Decode`'s own doc comment: "This function assumes that there is only one certificate and only one private key in the pfxData; if there are more use ToPEM instead." `LoadCertificate` always called `Decode` first and only reached its `ToPEM`-based `ExtraCerts` extraction if `Decode` had already succeeded — which, given the 2-bag requirement, only happens when there's nothing extra to extract. The `ExtraCerts` loop was unreachable dead code, not merely untested — a different, more serious finding than a coverage gap.
- **Confirmed live before fixing, not assumed:** `grep -rn "LoadCertificate" --include=*.go . | grep -v _test` showed `internal/documents/charter.go` (document signing) and `internal/export/pdf.go` (PDF export, via `app_documents.go`'s `productionPAdESExportRuntime`) both call it in production. Any commercially-issued signing certificate exported with its issuing chain bundled in — the normal shape for a real cert from a public CA, not a rare edge case — would fail `LoadCertificate` outright with "failed to decode P12: pkcs12: expected exactly two safe bags in the PFX PDU" instead of signing successfully with the chain attached. This reordered the work: the fix shipped in its own commit before the coverage pass continued, on the explicit reasoning that a live user-facing signing failure outranks the coverage schedule.
- **Fixture generation needed one non-obvious flag.** OpenSSL 3.x's `pkcs12 -export` defaults to AES-256-CBC/PBKDF2 encryption; `golang.org/x/crypto/pkcs12` is frozen and decode-only, supporting only the older RC2/3DES-based schemes. A fixture built without `-legacy` encodes fine in OpenSSL and fails to decode in Go with no useful error pointing at the cause. All three testdata fixtures (`internal/crypto/testdata/`, see its own `README.md`) were built with `-legacy`; this is recorded so a future maintainer regenerating them with a plain `openssl pkcs12 -export` doesn't hit the same confusing failure blind.
- **Committing them needed a scoped `.gitignore` exception, not a `-f` override.** `.gitignore` blanket-ignores `*.p12`/`*.pfx` under "NEVER commit private keys," and these fixtures are technically private key material even though self-signed and throwaway. `git add -f` was considered and rejected: an exception buried in one commit's flag is invisible to the next person and to every future `git status`. Instead `.gitignore` gained one narrow negation, `!internal/crypto/testdata/testonly-*.p12` — any other `.p12` anywhere in the tree, including a real certificate accidentally dropped into that same directory, stays ignored by default. `scripts/release-gate-scope-check.sh` gained a matching hard check: any tracked `.p12`/`.pfx` outside that exact allowlisted shape fails the release gate. Break-verified: a stray `internal/crypto/testdata/oops-not-testonly.p12` was correctly flagged and failed the check; removing it restored a clean pass.
- **Reproduced as a failing test before writing the fix, not after.** `TestLoadCertificate_ChainBundledInP12` was written and run against a hand-built 3-bag fixture (`testdata/testonly-rsa-3bag.p12`) while `LoadCertificate` still called `Decode` — it failed with exactly the "expected exactly two safe bags" error, serving simultaneously as the bug report and the fix's own break-verification: no synthetic mutation was needed afterward, because the mutation was the code that already existed. This is the same discipline this session applies to every test, just run in the opposite order (test first, fix second) because the "before" state was already broken rather than a working baseline to be mutated.
- **The rewrite:** `LoadCertificate` now calls `pkcs12.ToPEM` (no 2-bag limit) and two new unexported functions do the classification `Decode` used to do internally:
  - `parseP12Blocks` collects every `"PRIVATE KEY"`/`"CERTIFICATE"` PEM block `ToPEM` produces into one RSA key and a slice of certificates, rejecting non-RSA keys, multiple keys, and unparseable key/cert bytes explicitly rather than letting them through silently. `ToPEM`'s own doc comment notes `"PRIVATE KEY"`-typed blocks are PKCS#1-encoded for RSA and SEC 1-encoded for EC with no header distinguishing them, so both parsers are tried in order.
  - `splitLeafCertificate` identifies which certificate is the signer's own (vs. the issuing chain) by comparing each candidate's public key against the loaded private key's public key (`E` and `N` for RSA) — not the P12's optional `localKeyId` attribute, which the probe output showed some bags simply omit (the bundled "extra" certificate in the 3-bag fixture has `headers=map[]`, no `localKeyId` at all). A metadata-based match that happened to guess wrong would silently put an intermediate certificate in `Signer.Cert` and the real leaf in `ExtraCerts`, producing a CMS signature real-world PAdES validators reject — a wrong answer that looks like success. Public-key identity can't produce that failure mode: it only succeeds on an exact cryptographic match, and fails closed (a clear error) otherwise.
- **Regression discipline:** `testdata/testonly-rsa-2bag.p12` (a plain single-key, single-cert bundle — the shape every previously-working certificate has) is tested both for correctness and, in the same test, a full sign-and-verify round-trip through the loaded key (`SignPDFHash` + `rsa.VerifyPKCS1v15` against the loaded certificate's public key) — proving the loaded key material is actually usable, not just that parsing didn't error.
- **`LoadCertificate` sits at 92.3%, not 100% — recorded, not hidden.** The one remaining uncovered line is `LoadCertificate`'s propagation of `splitLeafCertificate`'s "no certificate matches this key" error. Forcing it needs a `.p12` whose bundled certificate doesn't match its own key; OpenSSL refuses to build one (`openssl pkcs12 -export -inkey leaf.key -in extra.crt` → "No cert in -in file 'extra.crt' matches private key"), so no realistic fixture reaches this exact line through `LoadCertificate` itself — only by hand-crafting DER bytes bypassing normal tooling, which wasn't judged worth doing for one error-propagation line whose underlying logic (`splitLeafCertificate`'s own "no match" branch) is already directly unit-tested and break-verified. Same class of decision as `internal/templates`' "first wall" entry above: a small, explicitly-recorded gap beats a forced or silently-skipped one.
- **Break-verified 3 targeted mutations** against the fix's actual logic — flipping the public-key match condition (`==` → `!=`, caught: both the chain-bundled and plain-2-bag tests failed with "no certificate matches"), removing the non-RSA rejection (caught: error text stopped mentioning "not RSA"), and removing the multiple-private-key rejection (caught) — on top of the red-test-first reproduction, which is itself the strongest possible break-verification for the core bug.
- **Scope, deliberately held:** this commit covers `LoadCertificate` and `SignPDFHash` only. `internal/crypto`'s remaining scattered 71–92% gaps in `encrypt.go`, `keywrap.go`, `pdf_cms.go`, and `pdf_cms_timestamp.go` are follow-up work, kept out so the bug-fix commit stays reviewable on its own rather than bundling a security-relevant behavior change with unrelated coverage additions.
- **Coverage ratchet updated:** go_default 8628/14605 (5977 uncovered) → 8674/14630 (5956 uncovered); go_duckdb 8706/14703 (5997 uncovered) → 8752/14728 (5976 uncovered). frontend unchanged.

### 2026-08-05 — Reviewed GO-2026-5932 (golang.org/x/crypto/openpgp): not reachable, no code or dependency change needed

- **The advisory:** `golang.org/x/crypto/openpgp` (and its `packet`/`armor`/`clearsign`/`errors`/`elgamal`/`s2k` subpackages) is flagged as unmaintained and unsafe by design, with `Fixed in: N/A` — this isn't a bug with a patch coming, it's a permanent "don't use this package" advisory against the package's own existence. `golang.org/x/crypto` is a direct dependency (`go.mod`), already at the latest available version (`go list -m -u golang.org/x/crypto` shows no update available — v0.54.0 is current), so there is no version bump that resolves this and running `go get -u` would have changed nothing while adding unrelated dependency churn to an otherwise-scoped coverage commit.
- **Confirmed not reachable, not assumed.** `grep -rn "x/crypto/" --include="*.go" .` (excluding `node_modules`) shows this repository imports exactly two `x/crypto` subpackages: `x/crypto/argon2` (password/key derivation in `internal/crypto/encrypt.go`) and `x/crypto/pkcs12` (P12 certificate loading in `internal/crypto/pdf_sign.go`, see the `LoadCertificate` entry above). `openpgp` is not among them. Running `govulncheck` against the full module (all packages, not the `. ./internal/...` scope this repo's other Go quality gates use — vulnerability scanning benefits from checking the whole dependency graph) confirms this with actual call-graph analysis, not just a version match: "Your code is affected by 0 vulnerabilities... 1 vulnerability in modules you require, but your code doesn't appear to call these vulnerabilities," and its verbose output shows `GO-2026-5932`'s only entry with `Found in: golang.org/x/crypto@v0.54.0` / `Fixed in: N/A` and no call stack — the tool sees the module in the graph but no path from GoPMgr's code into `openpgp`.
- **That same full-module `govulncheck` run exits 0 today** (checked directly, not assumed) — this advisory does not fail CI's `.github/workflows/ci.yml` "Vulnerability scan (govulncheck)" job (the `vuln` job, its "Scan default build" and "Scan DuckDB analytics engine" steps), which already runs on every push. That job is the actual gate for this class of issue; nothing new needed wiring it in, since it already exists and already passes cleanly with this advisory present in the dependency graph.
- **Forward-looking rule, so this doesn't get relitigated from scratch next time it's flagged:** if GoPMgr ever needs OpenPGP support, use `github.com/ProtonMail/go-crypto/openpgp` (the maintained drop-in fork the advisory itself recommends), never `golang.org/x/crypto/openpgp`.
- **No code change, no dependency change, no gate change.** This entry exists so a future `govulncheck`/dependency-audit run that resurfaces GO-2026-5932 finds a reviewed, dated decision instead of re-deriving the same investigation.

### 2026-08-05 — `internal/crypto`: covering `encrypt.go`/`keywrap.go`/`pdf_cms.go`/`pdf_cms_timestamp.go` (follow-up to the `LoadCertificate` fix)

- **A test helper's own bug, found by break-verifying it.** The first version of `encrypt_test.go`'s fake `rand.Reader` (used to force `EncryptBuffer`'s salt/nonce-generation error branches) delegated unconsumed reads via `io.ReadFull(rand.Reader, p)` from inside its own `Read` method — but `rand.Reader` was, at that point in the test, the swapped package var pointing at this same fake reader. Once past the one call it was designed to fail, every subsequent call recursed into itself via that swapped var, and a production-code mutation that removed the salt-error check turned "recurse once or twice" into infinite recursion: a real stack-overflow crash, not just a wrong test result. Fixed by capturing the genuine original `rand.Reader` in a struct field (`orig`) before swapping, and delegating to that captured value instead of re-reading the (still-swapped) package var. The general lesson: a fake that wraps a global by reading the same global back is a trap, even when the immediate test passes — this one only surfaced because the mutation drove execution down a path the original test never exercised alone.
- **A second, independent bug in the same helper: cascading failures made two tests indistinguishable from a passing one.** The first design used a "fails after N successful calls, then fails forever" counter. `TestEncryptBuffer_SaltGenerationFails` (0 successful calls) and `TestEncryptBuffer_NonceGenerationFails` (1 successful call) both passed — but break-verifying the salt-generation mutation showed the test still passed even with that specific error check deleted, because the *nonce* read (using the same permanently-broken reader) failed instead and produced the same observable error. Exactly the `applog`-session pattern (Phase 2's `applog` entry above) recurring in a new package. Fixed by switching to a "fail at exactly call N, succeed on every other call" design (`failingReaderAtCall`), so each test isolates precisely one `io.ReadFull` call — removing the salt check now lets execution reach a *successful* nonce read and returns `(ciphertext, nil)`, which the test correctly flags as unexpected.
- **`aes.NewCipher`'s and `DecryptBuffer`'s nonce-length error checks are kept, not deleted, and not tested — a different call than `internal/templates/jdm.go`'s dead-code deletions, and the distinction is worth stating precisely.** `jdm.go`'s deleted branches were dead *by type*: `json.RawMessage.MarshalJSON` cannot fail for any value of that type, ever, regardless of how the code around it changes. Here, `aes.NewCipher(key)` cannot fail *only because* `argonKeyLen` currently equals 32 — a package constant someone could edit — and `DecryptBuffer`'s "ciphertext too short for nonce" check is unreachable *only because* its sibling check's hardcoded `44` already encodes `saltLen+12+16` for the current, fixed GCM nonce/tag sizes. Both are value-relationship guarantees, not type-level ones: deleting either would leave production code exposed to a future edit silently breaking an invariant nothing else enforces. Kept, and documented in-place in `encrypt.go` with this exact reasoning, rather than deleted or left as an unexplained gap.
- **`cipher.NewGCM`'s error branch (both `EncryptBuffer` and `DecryptBuffer`) is real, not dead by any measure — and still not tested.** It only returns an error when the process runs under Go's FIPS 140-only enforcement mode (`crypto/fips140.Enforced()`), a process-global flag read at Go's crypto-package init, not re-evaluated per call — there's no test-local override that reaches it. Documented in-place rather than forced or silently left unexplained.
- **`keywrap.go`:** `GenerateDEK`'s `io.ReadFull(rand.Reader, ...)` failure closed with the same call-indexed fake reader as above (a single call this time, no cascading risk). `WrapKey`'s `EncryptBuffer` error-propagation line closed via an empty secret (`EncryptBuffer` already rejects that). `UnwrapKey`'s "not base64" branch closed directly. `UnwrapKey`'s post-decrypt "wrong DEK length" branch needed care routing around `WrapKey`: `WrapKey` itself rejects a wrong-length DEK before ever calling `EncryptBuffer`, so a test that built its wrapped blob via `WrapKey` would never reach `UnwrapKey`'s own length check. Built the blob directly with `EncryptBuffer` + `base64.StdEncoding.EncodeToString` instead, bypassing `WrapKey`'s own guard, so `UnwrapKey` receives a blob that decrypts successfully but to the wrong length — break-verified by removing `UnwrapKey`'s length check and confirming the test goes red only then, not before.
- **`pdf_cms.go` (new `pdf_cms_test.go`):** `cmsSignedAttributes`'s and `sortCMSAttributes`'s `asn1.Marshal` error branches are real, general-purpose error paths — reachable via a deliberately malformed input (a `pkcs7.Attribute.Value` of an unsupported Go type for the former; an empty `asn1.ObjectIdentifier` for the latter, both confirmed by direct experimentation before writing the tests, not assumed) — but never reachable through this package's own production call sites, which only ever construct well-formed attributes from real certificates and content digests. Tested directly against the unexported helpers with the malformed inputs, proving the error-handling code itself is real and correct, while every higher-level caller's propagation of that same error (`signDetachedPAdESCMS`'s three `if err != nil` wrappers around `cmsSignedAttributes`/`signCMSSignedAttributes`/`marshalCMSCertificates`, and `AddSignatureTimestamp`'s `sortCMSAttributes` wrapper) stays uncovered by design: the underlying failure is proven real, only the "what if a well-formed call site's dependency somehow failed anyway" wrapper line isn't independently exercisable without an artificial seam this package deliberately avoids (per the `LoadCertificate` entry's precedent — no crypto seams beyond the stdlib's own `rand.Reader`). Also checked and left undisclosed-but-real: `marshalCMSSignedAttributeSet`'s `asn1.Unmarshal` round-trip (parses bytes `asn1.Marshal` just produced two lines above — self-consistent DER, no forcing method found), `marshalCMSCertificateBytes`'s `asn1.Marshal(RawValue)` (empirically permissive of nil/empty/large-Tag/negative-Tag inputs in direct experimentation, no forcing method found), and `signCMSSignedAttributes`'s `rsa.SignPKCS1v15` (Go's own `rsa.GenerateKey` refuses keys below 1024 bits, comfortably above what PKCS#1v1.5/SHA-256 needs, so no key constructible through normal Go tooling can trigger its "message too long" case).
- **`pdf_cms_timestamp.go`:** `parseDetachedPAdESCMS`'s four previously-untested malformed-CMS branches (two "trailing data" checks, "not SignedData", "must contain detached id-data content", "no signature value") closed via hand-crafted DER, reusing the existing `appendSecondSignerForTest`-style pattern already in `pdf_cms_timestamp_test.go`: decode a real signed CMS, mutate one field, re-encode, assert the specific rejection. `AddSignatureTimestamp`'s remaining propagation lines (`sortCMSAttributes`, the two `asn1.Marshal` calls building the timestamped CMS) fall into the same "real but only reachable via malformed inputs no real call site produces" bucket as `pdf_cms.go` above.
- **Resulting coverage** (measured, not rounded up): `internal/crypto` 85.7% → 91.7%. Every remaining uncovered line traces to one of the named, disclosed reasons above — none left as an unexplained percentage.
- **Coverage ratchet updated:** go_default 8674/14630 (5956 uncovered) → 8687/14630 (5943 uncovered); go_duckdb 8752/14728 (5976 uncovered) → 8765/14728 (5963 uncovered). frontend unchanged.

### 2026-08-05 — `internal/admin`: 76.1% → 97.7%; a redundant downstream default masked a deleted-code mutation in the first draft

- **New forcing technique confirmed by direct experimentation: closing a `*db.Database` early.** Before writing any test, a throwaway probe (`zz_probe_test.go`, deleted after use) confirmed `db.Database.Close()` is idempotent (`if db == nil || db.Conn == nil { return nil }` — safe to call a second time from `t.Cleanup`) and that any subsequent method call against the closed handle returns a plain `sql: database is closed`. Used to force `SecureArchive`'s `GetSettings()` failure without a SQLite trigger — a simpler alternative for "the whole DB is unavailable" branches, complementing (not replacing) the existing trigger technique used for "one specific insert fails."
- **One test covered two branches at once, and the coverage report was checked (not assumed) to confirm both actually flipped.** Pointing a saved `CertPath` at a directory (same technique as `internal/db/backup_test.go`'s existing bundle-failure test) exercises `settings.CertPath != ""` on the way in and `CreateArchivalBundle`'s own error path on the way out — one test, two previously-uncovered lines. Confirmed via `go tool cover -html` that both lines flipped from red to green, not assumed from the test passing.
- **A redundant downstream default let a real mutation slip past the first draft of a test — caught only by break-verifying it.** `LogDocumentSignatureOutcome`'s `if signatureStatus == "" { signatureStatus = "unsigned" }` looks like it feeds `AppendAuditEvent`'s `audit_events.signature_status` column. It does — but `internal/db/audit.go`'s `appendAuditEventTx` *also* defaults `SignatureStatus == ""` to `"unsigned"` internally (line ~125), so a test asserting against `audit_events.signature_status` cannot tell "this package's default ran" from "this package's default was deleted and the DB layer's own default silently covered for it": deleting `workflow.go`'s check and re-running the test showed it still green. The only place this package's own defaulting is observable is `audit_log.details` — `LogAction`'s plain-text trail, built from the local `signatureStatus`/`details` variables *before* they reach `AppendAuditEvent`, with no re-defaulting downstream. Rewrote the assertion against `audit_log.details` and confirmed red under the same mutation. General lesson, adjacent to the cascading-failure trap named in the `applog` and `encrypt.go` entries above: a downstream layer that duplicates a guard for its own defense-in-depth reasons can just as easily hide the deletion of the same guard one layer up — trace the value all the way to an observable with no other writer, not just to the nearest table it touches.
- **`logSignatureCheckpointWithStatus`'s `json.Marshal` error check deleted, not tested — dead by type, matching `internal/templates/jdm.go`'s precedent, with the boundary condition stated in-code.** The struct marshaled is three plain `string` fields; `json.Marshal` cannot fail on that shape (invalid UTF-8 in a Go string is replaced during encoding, not rejected — there is no error return path to reach). Deleted per the established `jdm.go` precedent, with a comment noting the guarantee holds only while every field stays a `string` — a future field of type `any` or `map[string]any` would reintroduce a real, testable error path, unlike `encrypt.go`'s constant-dependent guards from the entry above, which are guarded by a comment instead of deleted because *their* failure mode depends on an editable constant rather than a fixed type shape.
- **One branch disclosed, not forced: `SecureArchive`'s `os.Remove` double-fault line.** After a failed audit-log write, `SecureArchive` tries to delete the just-created (now unaudited) archive; if *that* delete also fails, there's a distinct error-wrapping branch. Unlink permission in POSIX is governed by the parent directory's write bit, and making that directory non-writable would also block `CreateArchivalBundle` from creating the archive in the first place — so no single directory-permission state reaches this line without also short-circuiting the test earlier. A real proof of unreachability by ordinary means, not a shrug; documented in-place in `workflow.go` and in the ledger, in the same spirit as `applog`'s `pruneOldLogs` disclosure.
- **A log-only guard with no propagation admits no assertion distinguishing "ran" from "deleted" — second instance of this shape.** `logSignatureCheckpointWithStatus`'s `if err != nil { debug.Wrap(...) }` around `AppendAuditEvent` can be forced (a SQLite trigger on `audit_events`, matching the `audit_log` trigger technique already used for `SecureArchive`), so the statement is covered and the failure is proven real — but the guard neither propagates the error to its caller nor mutates any state beyond what the failed `AppendAuditEvent` call itself already didn't do, so deleting the guard entirely (`_, _ = s.DB.AppendAuditEvent(...)`) produces an identical observable: the blocked insert still leaves zero rows either way. Structurally the same as `applog`'s `pruneOldLogs` early-return disclosure. Recognizing the shape matters, not just naming it once: a first-pass draft of this test's own docstring claimed the row-absence assertion "distinguishes the guard running from it being gone," caught only on adversarial review — row absence is caused by the trigger blocking the insert, not by the guard's presence or absence, and a revised claim ("confirms the call was reached and failed, not skipped") still overreached until a second review pass removed "not skipped" too, since a never-called `AppendAuditEvent` is equally invisible to a count-based assertion. Test docstring now states only what the assertion actually proves; see `internal/admin/admin_test.go`.
- **Coverage ratchet updated:** go_default 8687/14630 (5943 uncovered) → 8694/14627 (5933 uncovered); go_duckdb 8765/14728 (5963 uncovered) → 8772/14725 (5953 uncovered). frontend unchanged.

### 2026-08-05 — `internal/users` (`dek.go` increment): 73.2% → 90.2% of `dek.go`; split the package rather than repeat the `internal/admin` review-load lesson

- **Package split decided before writing any code, not discovered mid-review.** `internal/users` (`dek.go` + `recovery.go` + `store.go`) has roughly 30 forceable branches across all three files — an order of magnitude more than `internal/admin`'s four, which itself needed two review passes to get its claims right. `/advisor`'s plan review flagged this directly: validate the forcing techniques on a small, self-contained unit first (`dek.go`: 9 items, 2 disclosures, zero dead-code decisions) before scaling them to `recovery.go`'s ~25. Same shape as the `internal/crypto` `LoadCertificate`-then-follow-up split.
- **A masked mutation in the first draft, same species as `internal/admin`'s — caught by the same discipline.** `TestUnlockDEK_GenerateDEKEntropyFailure`'s first draft asserted only `err != nil` after swapping in a failing `rand.Reader`. Deleting `UnlockDEK`'s `if err != nil { return nil, err }` guard on `crypto.GenerateDEK()`'s result does **not** produce a nil error: execution falls through to `crypto.WrapKey(dek, password)` with a nil-or-wrong-length `dek`, and `WrapKey` independently rejects it with `ErrBadDEK` — a different, but still non-nil, error. A bare `err != nil` check cannot tell "the guard ran and returned its own error" from "the guard is gone and a downstream guard caught the fallout instead." Found by break-verifying (deleting the guard, confirming the test *should* go red, finding it didn't), fixed by asserting the specific "generate DEK" substring instead. General form: any test built around `crypto.GenerateDEK()` → `crypto.WrapKey()` in sequence must pin the *first* call's own error text, because `WrapKey`'s length guard is a real, independent second line of defense that will mask a deleted first guard — worth remembering for `recovery.go`'s `IssueRecoveryCodes`, which has the identical `GenerateDEK`-adjacent-to-`WrapKey` shape in its loop.
- **`WrapKey`'s error branch forced without any entropy trickery, by construction, not by accident.** `crypto.EncryptBuffer` (called by `WrapKey`) rejects an empty password/secret before ever calling `rand.Reader` — confirmed by reading `encrypt.go` rather than assumed. So `UnlockDEK("alice", "")` reaches `WrapKey`'s error branch deterministically, with a precondition worth stating precisely: the account must be *fresh* (`wrapped_dek_pw == ""`), since any account that already has a real wrapped DEK routes through `UnwrapKey` instead, which never calls `WrapKey` at all — an empty-password call against an already-unlocked account would silently test the wrong branch.
- **New forcing technique, validated by a throwaway probe before the real test was written: drop the table, then call the migration method directly.** `migrateDEKColumns`'s ALTER-TABLE failure branch needed the column-presence probe (`PRAGMA table_info`) to report "absent" while the subsequent `ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN` still fails — contradictory-sounding until you drop the table entirely: `PRAGMA table_info` on a dropped table returns zero rows (so `present` stays `false`, same as a genuinely pre-ADR-001 database), and the following `ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN ...` then fails with "no such table". Confirmed directly (`DROP TABLE users` on a freshly-opened store, then call `s.migrateDEKColumns()`) before writing the real test, per this session's standing rule of probing forcing techniques before committing to them in a "real" test.
- **Two branches disclosed, not forced, and the reasoning is the same one used for `PRAGMA table_info` calls elsewhere in this codebase: SQLite's own contract makes them unreachable under normal operation.** `migrateDEKColumns`'s `rows.Scan` (on the six fixed pragma columns: int, text, text, int, any, int — always matching the Scan targets' types) and its post-loop `rows.Err()` (fires only on a mid-iteration cursor I/O error) have no portable forcing method short of a corrupted SQLite build. Documented in-place in `dek.go`.
- **Verified, not assumed, that `HasLegacyRecoveryCodeWraps` has a real production caller** before trusting `recovery.go`'s comment that a legacy-code password reset is "guaranteed" safe because "enabling encryption forces a code re-issue": `grep` confirmed `app_projects.go`'s `EncryptProjectAtRest` calls it and returns `ErrRecoveryCodesRequireReissue` when it reports `true`, so the claim holds and needed no correction — a check worth doing explicitly rather than trusting an in-code comment's claim on faith, per this session's step-3 documentation-accuracy requirement.
- **Fixed in passing: `dek.go`'s pre-existing `interface{}` → `any` linter hint**, in the same `migrateDEKColumns` function this change's Scan-error comment was added to (the `dflt interface{}` declaration sits a few lines above the comment, not on the same line). Mechanical, behavior-identical, same category as the Phase 2 `internal/users`/`main.go` linter fixes noted in an earlier entry above.
- **`recovery.go` and `store.go` remain for follow-up increments** — `recovery.go`'s own dead-code candidates (`RemainingRecoveryCodes`'s unreachable `sql.ErrNoRows` branch on a bare `COUNT(*)`, and `generateCode`'s `len(enc) < 16` check) were identified during planning but **not** acted on yet, and were initially miscategorized: first proposed for deletion under the `internal/templates/jdm.go` "dead by type" precedent, corrected by `/advisor` to the `encrypt.go`/`argonKeyLen` "dead by an editable adjacent constant/expression" precedent instead — `RemainingRecoveryCodes`'s `COUNT(*)` query is what makes `ErrNoRows` unreachable, not any property of the return type itself, so both should be kept-and-documented-in-place when that increment lands, not deleted.
- **Coverage ratchet updated:** go_default 8694/14627 (5933 uncovered) → 8702/14627 (5925 uncovered); go_duckdb 8772/14725 (5953 uncovered) → 8780/14725 (5945 uncovered). frontend unchanged.

### 2026-08-05 — `internal/users` (`recovery.go` increment 1 of 2): 70.5% → 83.0% of `recovery.go`; split by function this time, not just by file

- **Split one level finer than the `dek.go`/`recovery.go`/`store.go` file split, for a technique-specific reason.** `/advisor`'s plan review: a call-indexed `rand.Reader` fake was needed for two different functions in `recovery.go` (`IssueRecoveryCodes` and, for increment 2, `ResetWithRecoveryCode`), at two different call indices, and the correctness of each index rested on a call count derived by reading source rather than measuring it. Validate the fake on the simpler function (`IssueRecoveryCodes`) first, with the index confirmed empirically, before trusting it on the more complex one. This increment: `migrateRecoveryTable`, `RemainingRecoveryCodes`, `IssueRecoveryCodes` (9 branches). `ResetWithRecoveryCode` (12 branches) is increment 2.
- **Measured the rand call sequence with a throwaway counting reader instead of trusting the source read, per `/advisor`'s explicit instruction — and it's a good thing this was checked, not assumed.** A wrapper recording `(call#, len(p))` and delegating via `io.ReadFull(orig, p)` (never a raw, possibly-short `Read` — a short read would make `io.ReadFull` call back in and double-count one logical read as two, silently pointing a call-indexed fake at the wrong consumer) confirmed `IssueRecoveryCodes`' per-iteration sequence exactly as predicted: call 1 = `generateCode` (10 bytes), call 2 = `HashPassword`'s salt (16 bytes), calls 3–4 = `WrapKey`'s salt+nonce (16, 12 bytes) when `dek != nil`. The same probe run against `ResetWithRecoveryCode` (deferred to increment 2, but run now while the probe was already built) confirmed its non-legacy path is exactly 3 calls: `WrapKey`'s salt+nonce (calls 1–2), then `HashPassword`'s salt (call 3) — the index increment 2's call-indexed fake will target.
- **A masked-mutation risk named in the plan, and confirmed present, on the same `GenerateDEK`-adjacent-to-`WrapKey` shape flagged during the `dek.go` increment.** `TestIssueRecoveryCodes_GenerateCodeEntropyFailure` (always-fail reader) and `TestIssueRecoveryCodes_HashPasswordEntropyFailure` (call-indexed, fails only call 2) assert *different* substrings (`"recovery: read entropy"` vs `"read salt"`) specifically so that deleting one guard can't produce a result the other test would also accept. Both break-verified individually: deleting `generateCode`'s error check inside the loop makes `auth.HashPassword("")` fail on its own *empty-password* check (a third, distinct error) rather than continuing to rand — confirming the two tests are watching genuinely different failure points, not two names for the same one.
- **`crypto.WrapKey`'s wrong-length-DEK rejection (`ErrBadDEK`) forced the in-loop `WrapKey` branch deterministically, no entropy tricks needed** — same technique class as the `dek.go` increment's empty-password `WrapKey` forcing, applied here via `len(dek) != 32` instead of an empty secret.
- **A real SQLite behavior discovered empirically, not assumed, and it broke the first draft of a trigger test — the draft failed against correct, unmutated code, not just under a later mutation.** `BEFORE DELETE` triggers only fire for rows a `DELETE` statement actually matches; a fresh account has no recovery codes yet, so `DELETE FROM recovery_codes WHERE username = ?` against it is a silent no-op that never reaches a trigger at all. `TestIssueRecoveryCodes_DeleteFailsOnBlockedTrigger`'s first draft installed the trigger on a fresh account and ran red immediately (`error = <nil>, want the delete trigger's abort message`) even with `IssueRecoveryCodes` untouched, because the trigger genuinely never fired. Fixed by issuing codes once first, so the *second* `IssueRecoveryCodes` call has a real row for the trigger to block. Named here as a reusable lesson: any `BEFORE DELETE`/`BEFORE UPDATE` trigger-based fault-injection test in this codebase needs a real target row already present, not just a schema that could hold one.
- **Two DELETE/INSERT triggers and two UPDATE triggers (this increment's two, plus `store_test.go`'s two from `dek.go`) all use distinct `RAISE(ABORT, ...)` messages, per `/advisor`'s explicit requirement** — otherwise e.g. `TestIssueRecoveryCodes_DeleteFailsOnBlockedTrigger` and `TestIssueRecoveryCodes_InsertFailsOnBlockedTrigger` would be mutually substitutable: a bug that broke the INSERT branch but left the DELETE-blocking test's generic `err != nil` assertion in place would go unnoticed. Break-verified individually — see the two mutations in `recovery_test.go`.
- **Two branches disclosed, not forced, in `IssueRecoveryCodes`.** `tx.Begin()`: closed-DB is the technique used everywhere else in this file, but `IssueRecoveryCodes`' first DB call is the user-existence `QueryRow`, three lines before `tx.Begin()` — closing the store trips that earlier check and can never isolate `Begin()` specifically. `tx.Commit()`: no portable forcing method available in SQLite/`database/sql` without a deferred-constraint or disk-level fault this package has no injection point for.
- **Runtime cost noted per `/advisor`'s flag, not yet acted on.** `internal/users`' full suite grew from ~3.3s (pre-Phase-2) to ~4.5s across the `dek.go` and this `recovery.go` increment combined — `IssueRecoveryCodes` runs 8 real Argon2id hashes at 64 MiB per call, and several new tests call it multiple times. Not a problem yet; worth checking again after `ResetWithRecoveryCode` and `store.go` add their own tests, since each `ResetWithRecoveryCode` call is itself 2 more Argon2id hashes.
- **Coverage ratchet updated:** go_default 8702/14627 (5925 uncovered) → 8716/14627 (5911 uncovered); go_duckdb 8780/14725 (5945 uncovered) → 8794/14725 (5931 uncovered). frontend unchanged.

### 2026-08-05 — `internal/users` (`recovery.go` increment 2 of 2): 83.0% → 92.9% of `recovery.go`; two masked-mutation findings in the same session as the fake reader that enabled them

- **The call-indexed `rand.Reader` fake, validated in increment 1, reused at the index its own probe had already confirmed (index 3, `ResetWithRecoveryCode`'s `HashPassword` salt read on the non-legacy path) — no new measurement needed, only the precondition re-stated in the test's docstring** (index 3 assumes the non-legacy path specifically, since a legacy code would insert an extra `GenerateDEK` call ahead of `WrapKey`'s two and silently shift the target index by one).
- **New forcing technique, probe-confirmed before the real test: corrupt only `wrapped_dek`, leave `code_hash` untouched.** `TestResetWithRecoveryCode_RejectsCorruptedWrap` — arguably the single most important test in this file — issues a real DEK-wrapped code, then directly `UPDATE`s only the `wrapped_dek` column to garbage. `VerifyPassword` still matches (the hash wasn't touched), so `matchWrap` is populated and `crypto.UnwrapKey` fails deterministically on the corrupted blob, hitting `"users: recovery wrap corrupt: %w"` — the branch that stops a corrupted or tampered wrap from being silently treated as a legacy no-wrap code (which would generate a *fresh* DEK and orphan every encrypted project the user has). No entropy tricks needed; base64/ciphertext validation fails on its own.
- **Masked mutation #1, same cascade shape as `dek.go`'s `GenerateDEK`-into-`WrapKey` finding, new specifics: `WrapKey`'s failure cascades into `HashPassword`'s.** `TestResetWithRecoveryCode_WrapKeyEntropyFailure`'s first draft asserted only `err != nil`. Deleting `WrapKey`'s guard doesn't make `ResetWithRecoveryCode` succeed — three lines later, `auth.HashPassword(newPassword)` independently reads the same broken `rand.Reader` for its own salt and fails too, just with different text (`EncryptBuffer` propagates its salt-read error bare; `HashPassword` wraps its own with `"auth: read salt: %w"`). Fixed by asserting the *exact* bare string. General lesson, recurring pattern this session (`encrypt.go`, `applog`, `dek.go`, now here): whenever two guards in sequence both ultimately read the same fallible resource (here, `rand.Reader`; in `dek.go` and `encrypt.go`, also `rand.Reader`; in `applog`, the log directory), a test targeting the *first* guard must assert something the *second* guard's failure text can't also satisfy.
- **Masked mutation #2, a genuinely new shape: not a cascade, a deliberate design choice that makes a guard's presence unobservable.** `TestResetWithRecoveryCode_RejectsInvalidUsername`'s assertion (`err == ErrInvalidRecoveryCode`) stayed green after deleting `ValidateUsername`'s guard — not because of a downstream guard catching the same failure differently, but because `ResetWithRecoveryCode` *deliberately* returns the identical `ErrInvalidRecoveryCode` sentinel for both "malformed username" and "well-formed username, no matching code," per `ErrInvalidRecoveryCode`'s own doc comment: indistinguishable by design, to prevent an attacker from using the error to enumerate valid usernames. With the guard deleted, the malformed username simply matches zero rows in the following `SELECT`, falls through to `matchID < 0`, and returns the *same* sentinel via a different path — no assertion on the returned error, however specific, can ever tell these two paths apart, because the whole point of the design is that they shouldn't be tellable apart. Resolution: keep the test (it still pins a real, valuable behavioral guarantee — invalid usernames are always rejected), but stop calling it a guard-presence test. Its docstring and the ledger entry now say precisely what it does and doesn't prove, same disclosure discipline as `applog`'s `pruneOldLogs` test and `internal/admin`'s log-only-guard test, but for a third distinct underlying reason: swallowed error (`applog`), no propagation (`admin`), deliberate error unification (here).
- **`internal/users`' `dek.go` and `recovery.go` are now complete for Phase 2 purposes** — every remaining uncovered line in both files traces to a named, disclosed reason (SQLite pragma/cursor guarantees, no portable COMMIT-failure injection, or — new this round — deliberate anti-enumeration error unification). `store.go` (75.5%) is the last increment for this package.
- **Coverage ratchet updated:** go_default 8716/14627 (5911 uncovered) → 8727/14627 (5900 uncovered); go_duckdb 8794/14725 (5931 uncovered) → 8805/14725 (5920 uncovered). frontend unchanged.

### 2026-08-05 — `internal/users` (`store.go` increment, sub-increment 1 of 2 — DB-backed functions): 75.5% → 87.7% of `store.go`; `/advisor`'s plan review flagged two masking risks before any code was written, both confirmed by probe

- **`/advisor` split `store.go` into two sub-increments up front rather than letting the split get discovered mid-implementation**, because it mixes two forcing-technique families in one file: DB-backed functions (closed this round) and filesystem-backed ones (`copyTree`'s symlink/irregular-file arms, `ensurePrivateDir`'s chmod failures — deferred). Drawing the boundary at function level, not technique level, mattered: `Open` itself uses both an `ensurePrivateDir` call and DB calls, so it stayed in this round rather than being split across both.
- **Every forcing technique was probed empirically before being written into a real test** — the standing rule this session, reconfirmed to pay off again. Probes covered: root-dir-as-file and system.db-as-directory collisions for `Open`; a second `Open` on the same rootDir to hit `migrateAdminColumn`'s already-present branch; `SetAdmin`/`DeleteAccount` on a nonexistent user; and — the two genuinely new techniques this round — `UPDATE users SET is_admin = 'not-an-int'` (SQLite's type affinity stores a non-convertible value into an `INTEGER`-typed column instead of rejecting the write, so the later `Scan(&isAdmin)` fails deterministically with no closed connection or trigger needed) and `t.Setenv("XDG_DATA_HOME", ...)` (confirmed `legacyRootCandidates()` reads the env var fresh on every call rather than caching it at package-init time, making `MigrateLegacyRoot`'s real candidate-resolution path fully test-controllable — validated for the next sub-increment, not used yet).
- **Masked mutation #1: `TestOpen_RootDirIsFile` is a cascade, not a bare pass/fail.** First draft would have asserted only `err != nil`. Deleting `Open`'s `ensurePrivateDir` guard doesn't make `Open` succeed — `sql.Open` connects lazily and never touches the filesystem itself, so execution falls through to `s.migrate()`'s first `Exec` against `<rootDir-as-file>/system.db`, which fails with an unrelated "unable to open database file" error. Same cascading-fallible-path shape as every other instance this session, this time caught by `/advisor` reading the plan and predicting it, then confirmed by direct mutation before the assertion was finalized — fixed by asserting the specific `"mkdir root"` wrapper text and confirming the mutation produces `"...migrate: unable to open database file..."` instead, with no `"mkdir root"` substring.
- **Masked mutation #2, a second instance of `recovery.go`'s anti-enumeration-unification shape, but this one turned out to be break-verifiable where that one wasn't.** `Authenticate`'s `ValidateUsername` guard: on an open store, a present guard and a deleted one both return the identical `ErrNoSuchUser` — present via the guard itself, deleted via `sql.ErrNoRows` from the fallthrough `SELECT` (per `ErrNoSuchUser`'s own merge-in-UI doc comment) — so `/advisor` correctly flagged that a plain equality check on an open store would be masked, confirmed by a direct probe showing exactly that (the mutated code still returns `ErrNoSuchUser` on an open store). But `/advisor` also spotted the discriminator `recovery.go`'s version lacked: the guard short-circuits *before any DB access*, so closing the store first makes the two paths diverge on *when* the connection gets touched, not just on the return value. `TestAuthenticate_ValidateUsernameShortCircuitsBeforeDBAccess` closes the store, then asserts the invalid-username call still returns `ErrNoSuchUser` (present guard: never touches the closed connection) rather than `"database is closed"` (deleted guard: reaches the closed connection via the fallthrough `QueryRow`). Confirmed both directions: the closed-store test goes red under the mutation, and a throwaway probe confirmed an open-store equality check alone would have stayed green under the identical mutation.
- **`CreateAccount`, `Authenticate`, and `List` reach 100%.** Seven other functions (`Open`, `Close`, `RootDir`, `migrate`, `migrateAdminColumn`, `SetAdmin`, `DeleteAccount`) have documented, disclosed-untested remainders, each with an in-code comment at the point of the check: `Open`'s own `sql.Open` error and both migrate/private-file close-error cascades (no portable way to fail `conn.Close()` itself after an already-failed statement on the same connection); `migrate`'s `migrateRecoveryTable`/`migrateDEKColumns` propagation checks (no hook between them and the prior successful `Exec` on the same live connection, and SQLite triggers don't fire on the CREATE TABLE/ALTER TABLE DDL those functions issue — a closed connection trips the schema `Exec` first, every time); `migrateAdminColumn`'s PRAGMA-cursor `Scan`/`rows.Err()` (same class as `dek.go`'s `migrateDEKColumns` disclosure — SQLite guarantees `PRAGMA table_info`'s six-column shape); `SetAdmin`'s and `DeleteAccount`'s admin-COUNT query errors (same live-connection, no-DML-trigger-possible reasoning as `migrate`'s propagation checks — a `SELECT` can't be sabotaged with a trigger the way an `INSERT`/`UPDATE`/`DELETE` can).
- **Coverage ratchet updated:** go_default 8727/14627 (5900 uncovered) → 8754/14627 (5873 uncovered); go_duckdb 8805/14725 (5920 uncovered) → 8832/14725 (5893 uncovered). frontend unchanged.
- **Same-day self-critique correction, before starting sub-increment 2:** `/advisor` caught three issues in the just-committed work — a comment in `store.go` pointing at `TestEnsurePrivateSQLiteFiles_MainPathMissing`, a test name that didn't exist yet (planned for the next sub-increment, since `ensurePrivateSQLiteFiles` is nominally a filesystem-backed function); and a ROADMAP sentence claiming `syscall.Mkfifo` was "already probe-confirmed" for the next sub-increment when only the Unix-socket variant had actually been run. Rather than reword the dangling comment, added the named test now — its forcing technique (a nonexistent target path) was already probe-confirmed and needed no filesystem-obstacle machinery from the next sub-increment, so it was cleaner to close for real than to defer. Break-verified the same way as every other test this increment: removing the guard (`_ = os.Chmod(...)` instead of checking its error) makes the test go red, restore diffs clean. This closed `ensurePrivateSQLiteFiles` to 83.3% (one branch, the non-ENOENT sidecar chmod, stays disclosed-untested) and, as a side effect already true before this correction, revealed `ensurePrivateDir` was already at 100% — `TestOpen_RootDirIsFile`'s root-as-file collision forces exactly that function's guard. `store.go`: 87.7% → 88.2%; package: 89.5% → 89.8%. Also ran the `syscall.Mkfifo` probe for real (confirmed it lands in `copyTree`'s `default:` skip branch, as predicted) so the ROADMAP's claim for the next sub-increment is now measured rather than merely predicted from `sun_path`-length reasoning.

### 2026-08-05 — `internal/users` (`store.go` increment, sub-increment 2 of 2 — filesystem-backed functions): 88.2% → 93.6% of `store.go` (89.8% → 93.0% of the package); `internal/users` now closed out entirely, every remaining uncovered line in the package traces to a named, disclosed reason

- **`/advisor`'s plan review, before any code was written, flagged six items across the nine planned tests as masking risks — four cascade-prone assertions and two branches with no possible mutation at all — and specified the exact fix (or the exact reason no fix exists) for each, all confirmed by direct mutation or direct reasoning before finalizing, none discovered the hard way during break-verify.** This is the plan-review step paying off maximally: every masking risk this increment was caught and fixed before a single real assertion was written, rather than after a break-verify run went unexpectedly green.
- **All four `copyFile` tests are cascade-prone by construction, not by accident.** Every guard in `copyFile` leaves a nil `*os.File` behind when deleted (`in, err := os.Open(src)` deleted → `in` is nil; `out, err := os.OpenFile(dst, ...)` deleted → `out` is nil), and Go's `*os.File` has nil-receiver methods that return `os.ErrInvalid` ("invalid argument") rather than panicking — so a downstream guard always produces *some* non-nil error, satisfying any bare `err != nil` check regardless of which guard actually ran. `TestCopyFile_MkdirAllFails` asserts the substring "mkdir": probing showed the real error and the cascaded error happen to share the substring "not a directory," so the assertion was written narrow enough ("mkdir...") to discriminate them anyway, confirmed by mutation. `TestCopyFile_SourceOpenFails` asserts "no such file or directory" (the cascade produces the disjoint "invalid argument" instead). `TestCopyFile_DestOpenFails` asserts "is a directory" (same disjoint-cascade shape, different guard). `TestCopyFile_IoCopyFails` is deliberately left as a bare non-nil check — no text pin — because deleting *that* guard doesn't cascade: `in`/`out` are both still valid, non-nil handles by that point (the earlier two guards did their job), so `io.Copy`'s own failure (reading from a directory) is what would have to occur, and skipping the check just falls through to a valid, empty `out.Close()` call — genuinely `nil`, the one real break-verification in the set. Pinning platform text there was deliberately avoided: Linux's `io.Copy` may route a directory-read attempt through `copy_file_range` rather than produce macOS's literal "read ...: is a directory" wording, so a text pin would be non-portable across this repo's actual CI matrix (ubuntu + macOS, confirmed via `.github/workflows/*.yml`) even though it works locally.
- **`copyTree`'s symlink-skip and `default:`-skip arms are a fourth distinct disclosed-limit shape: two switch arms with an identical observable body.** Deleting the symlink case's `return nil` doesn't change behavior at all — a symlink's `info.Mode().IsRegular()` is false (the mode comes from `d.Info()`, which is Lstat-based and carries `ModeSymlink`), so execution falls through to the `default:` arm and returns the same `nil` either way. And `default:` itself can't be deleted without leaving the `switch` without a fallthrough return, a compile error, not a silent behavior change. `TestCopyTree_SkipsSymlinksAndIrregularFiles` is kept — "symlinks and irregular files are never copied into a fresh user's data directory" is a real, security-relevant contract (a legacy install could plausibly contain a symlink pointing outside the data root) — but its docstring and the ledger entry say precisely that it pins behavior, not that it proves either arm's presence, matching the disclosure discipline established for `applog`'s swallowed-error test, `internal/admin`'s no-propagation test, and `recovery.go`'s deliberate-error-unification test.
